"Expectation became the threat," Lin said.

"Wanting," Mara replied. "They criminalized wanting before it could gather."

Attached minutes showed the vote: five to four. The four dissenters proposed divided custody and ward witnesses. Within a year, two entered the clean-up registry. One recanted. The fourth became an Archivist.

Cael looked at the old man. "Your office came from the losing vote."

"Partly. It was given custody but no authority to disclose. A compromise designed to preserve evidence while making use of it nearly impossible."

"And you accepted that limit for forty-three years."

"Yes."

The old man's repeated admission did not become less severe. Lin appreciated that he never translated confession into a request for absolution.

They opened the office charter. Its four roles had been compressed through successive amendments. Reader moved to the Hall. Witness became a seal rather than a person. Closer belonged to the Weaver-Prime. Carrier disappeared entirely because common memory assumed no individual could choose destination.

The Archive retained source without the means to convene the others.

"The marks around the red door remember an institution no longer present," Sef said.

Lin copied every amendment. The history of the mechanism mattered as much as the moral claim. A future guild could restore four ceremonial palms while leaving real authority concentrated. The Return would need separate keys, budgets, training, and public removal processes for each role.

Mara asked who had paid for the vault during its hidden years.

The Archivist showed them maintenance allocations disguised as moisture control in public shelves. Citizens funded secrecy without knowing. Lin added fiscal custody to the map. A hidden office remained hidden partly because no public body could trace what resources kept it alive.

Cael found his own wages in those accounts.

"Red-room preservation assistant," he read. "That is me."

"On paper," the Archivist said.

"Then the council paid me to guard a door it said did not exist."

The absurdity made him laugh once, sharply. No one joined him.

They prepared a public ledger of the concealed costs. Evidence of crime should include the ordinary administrative hands that sustained it, not to punish clerks who moved linen but to prevent future concealment from hiding inside harmless categories.

Before Lin could close the accounts, the junior clerk knocked from beyond the red door. Three measured taps, a pause, then one more. The Archivist recognized the old request for witnessed entry. He looked toward Mara rather than answering for her.

"Let her ask," Mara said.

Sef worked the closing plate while Lin held the reader's mark. Cael remained carrier. Mara opened the witness slit, a narrow iron panel Lin had mistaken for ventilation. One grey eye appeared at its height.

"There are preservation deliveries due at first light," the clerk said. "If I mark them refused, Hall review follows. If I accept them, two porters enter this room. Which record do you want made?"

The question exposed a consequence their morning had not mapped. Opening custody did not suspend mildew, wages, keys, or scheduled labor. A clean heroic breach would leave ordinary workers carrying unexplained deviations.

Lin asked what the delivery contained.

"Sedge rolls, lamp salt, six linen bolts. The invoice names moisture control."

Cael found the matching allocation. One bolt belonged to common shelves; five had been charged to the concealed room. The false category hid more than money. It directed materials away from public records whose wrappings showed brown rot at the corners.

"Can the porters wait in the anteroom?" Lin asked.

"For one quarter bell. After that they lose the morning cart."

Mara touched the witness aperture. "Do they know what this door holds?"

"They know it takes five bolts and gives none back. Workers notice weight even when ledgers rename it."

The Archivist lowered his head. Secrecy had demanded the clerks know enough to serve it while forbidding them the knowledge needed to object.

Lin drew a temporary procedure in the margin of the cost ledger. Delivery would be received at the outer desk. The clerk would verify quantity. Cael would carry one bolt into the vault after recording destination openly. Four bolts would go to damaged common rolls pending a citizens' custody decision. No porter would cross the red door without informed agreement to witness what lay inside.

"You are reallocating council property," the Archivist said.

"You allocated it under a false name," Cael replied.

"I am identifying the challenge the Hall will make."

Lin kept writing. "Then attach your original authorization and my diversion. Let both hands remain visible."

They admitted the clerk to the central table. Her name, Iven Sal, appeared in the maintenance book for nine years, always beside totals approved by the Archivist. Lin explained the forged surveys, the clean-up registry, and the four limited roles. Iven listened with one palm spread over an ink stain. She asked no question about the rebellion. Her first concern involved the public shelves.

"How many rolls did we lose while this room received dry linen?"

The Archivist answered without looking at the damaged-copies index. "Seventy-two beyond repair. Another hundred and nine partly legible."

Iven inhaled through her nose. "I filed every loss as age."

"You filed the cause I gave you."

"Under my hand."

Nobody relieved her of that fact. Mara asked what choices the clerk had possessed when she signed. Iven could question a count, refuse a damaged shipment, or report theft. She could not inspect the sealed destination or challenge an Archivist's preservation classification. Her signature created apparent oversight without access.

Lin added a fifth requirement beneath the restored offices: any witness asked to certify expenditure must see both origin and destination. It did not create a fifth hand at the vault. It kept financial witness from becoming decoration.

The delivery arrived while Iven read the copied border. Wheels scraped in the anteroom. A porter complained about the stairs. Life pressed against the proof with damp boots and a schedule.

Cael went out to explain the changed distribution. One porter left rather than witness a sealed room's purpose. The other, an older woman with salt scars on both wrists, accepted after Iven read the terms aloud. She carried the single bolt only to the threshold. Cael took it from there. Carrier authority remained ordinary enough to include linen.

When he returned, he found Lin comparing shelf numbers against the registry codes. Three destinations did not correspond to any rack. The Archivist led them to a floor panel behind the disputed surveys. Beneath it lay packets of witness objections, each refused entry to common record because its source had been removed before review.

They did not open the private packets. Iven read names from the outer seals while Mara checked them against the registry. Eleven matched. Two carried no listed removal. One seal had been broken and repaired with later wax.

"Who opened this?" Lin asked.

The Archivist turned the packet under lamplight. "My predecessor's seal. I cannot tell whether he read or merely inspected it."

Uncertainty received an open hook. Lin would not turn missing provenance into accusation. She recorded wax color, fracture, repair, and every person now present. The packet returned unopened beneath separate witness cord.

At the outer desk, porters rolled four fresh bolts toward the common shelves. Clerks began lifting damaged records from their racks. The redistribution would be visible before the council heard a word of testimony. It would also invite questions they could no longer contain.

Iven signed the new ledger entry, then pushed the pen toward the Archivist.

"Not approval," she said. "Acknowledgment that your prior order caused the diversion."

He signed.

Lin signed as reader of the allocation. Mara verified the explanation. Cael marked the changed destination. Sef closed the entry against further amendment without four-role review.

For the first time that morning, restored authority altered something beyond the table. Five bolts no longer disappeared into a harmless heading. One hidden packet carried a public uncertainty mark. A clerk could point to the line she had been denied power to question.

The silence had become permanent through amendment, not founding design.

That mattered. A system often claimed ancient necessity when the actual lock had been added by frightened successors.

Lin mapped the chain of changes. Mara tested each against her inherited testimony. Sef translated notation that the later guild had stopped teaching. The Archivist supplied custody. Cael carried the petition and interrupted whenever adult abstraction erased a person.

By sunrise they had assembled a record no single office could own:

The House forged the grievance.

The Weave ended the resulting wars.

The Silence prevented inherited vengeance but removed future consent.

The rebellion sought reconsideration.

The guild folded its history.

The clean-up hid removals beneath the language of mercy.

Some custodians preserved enough evidence for a later challenge, while others, including the Archivist, kept that evidence inaccessible long after immediate danger passed.

No part absolved another.

The Archivist rolled the original maps and replaced them on the rack. "Take the copies. Leave the sources. If the Hall seizes one, the other remains."

"Come with us," Cael said.

The old man looked toward the red door. "To the council?"

"To the alcove first. You keep talking as if your duty lives in this room."

"It has."

"Then move it."

A smile nearly reached the Archivist's mouth. It failed under the weight of the morning.

"I will testify," he said. "I will also surrender the cord. Custody concentrated in one person helped make this possible. The red room should return to four keys."

Mara shook her head. "Not symbolic hands. Actual offices with power to refuse one another."

"Reader, witness, closer, carrier," Sef said.

Lin added them to the provenance map. Beside each role she left a blank for the Hold to fill.

They opened the red door onto morning.

A junior clerk stood in the anteroom with a stack of damp rolls. She saw the Archivist emerge behind Lin, saw Cael carrying a wrapped source, and lowered the rolls without being asked.

"Hall review?" she said.

"Cancelled," the Archivist replied. "There will be another kind."

He removed the black cord from his throat and placed it on the ledger desk. The gesture changed nothing outside the Archive. No bell sounded. No guild officer witnessed it. Yet Cael watched, and Mara watched, and Sef touched the desk to fix the location in memory. Lin drew the cord as the final mark on the morning's map.

They left the sources locked but the chain unfastened. The red door closed under four separate hands. Lin read. Mara witnessed. Sef turned the closing plate. Cael carried the copied testimony out.

The Archivist kept no hand on the lock.

In the street, the Hold continued its unremembered morning. Bread carts crossed the square. The western well opened. A patrol passed without recognizing the old man outside his proper room.

The Archivist squinted into the light. For the first time in Lin's memory of him, he had nothing in his hands.

Cael shifted his mother's petition beneath one arm and offered him the other.

The old man took it.

Together they walked toward Sef's alcove, while Lin carried the provenance map and the original forged border remained behind the red door, no longer alone with the person who had been afraid to open it.

Chapter 14 — The Keeper Speaks

Sef knew the next Threshold would take him because onions had stopped smelling like his wife.

They still smelled sharp when he cut them. His eyes watered. His tongue remembered sweetness after the pan warmed. What had vanished was Elian at twenty-six, laughing because he had burned the first onion in their new room.

The fact of the memory remained. Its life had gone.

He sat in his alcove before dawn and arranged the four hand stones on the table. One for reading, one for witnessing, one for closing, one for carrying. Each had been cut for an ordinary palm. The work had once required four people because the old guild distrusted any keeper who could open, interpret, seal, and transport a record alone.

Later guilds drew the four marks as one symbol and taught it as decoration. Power preferred a mystery to a limit.

Lin arrived with the provenance map from the vault. Mara followed, then Cael with his mother's petition, then the Archivist without his black cord. Sef made the old man wait by the curtain.

"You may witness," he told him. "You do not keep today."

The Archivist bowed and took the wall stool.

Sef gave the reader's stone to Lin.

"No," she said. "You read the old script."

"Today you do."

Her refusal tightened. He had seen the same expression in young Cartographers asked to accept rank before they trusted their skill. The guild encouraged that uncertainty when it made obedience easier and punished it when it slowed work.

"I know fragments," she said.

"Then learn sequence. Fragments become craft when the order survives the person."

He spread the oldest border-map beneath the stones. The line did not describe a wall. It followed the Saltmarsh's shifting edge, curved beneath the Hold, and returned through the Underloom seam. The map's diagonal changed with the reader's angle.

Lin placed her hand where Sef directed.

"Do not press downward," he said. "A map is not a door you force. Let the surface show where it resists you."

She reduced the pressure.

The border lifted through the stone. Upper Hold, folded substrate, outer settlements, and the marsh appeared as one connected field. The modern guild map ended at the ward wall. The old one crossed it.

Mara inhaled when her settlement routes emerged at the eastern edge.

"The wider world never vanished," Sef said. "The Hall stopped including it."

Lin followed the line with her eyes. "Why draw the border if it isn't a wall?"

"To mark a change in responsibility. Inside, the Weave carries memory. Outside, people carry one another. Neither makes the other unreal."

He turned the reader's stone one quarter.

The image closed.

"Closing does not mean erasing," he said. "Say it back."

"Closing preserves a record outside immediate use."

"Again."

"A closed map remains present."

"Again."

Impatience entered her voice. "Closed is not ended."

The live thread answered beneath the floor, three short pulses and one held.

Sef smiled. "Now the stone believes you."

They worked through the morning. Lin learned the angle for a witness copy, which retained the source's disagreements rather than smoothing them into one line. She learned to mark uncertainty with an open hook instead of an empty space. She learned why the old Cartographers placed source, reader, and date inside the map rather than on a removable label.

Every lesson corrected a habit the modern Hall had trained into her.

When she reached for a single clean border, Sef made her preserve both seasonal positions. When she called Mara's inherited account unreliable, he made her distinguish changed detail from stable geometry. When she described the pre-Silence map as proof, he asked proof of what, made by whom, in relation to which competing claim.

"Clean maps breed clean lies," he said. "The world makes edges with weather, bodies, custom, fear. If a line looks effortless, find the hand that benefited from smoothing it."

The Archivist shifted on the stool. Sef saw the lesson reach him too late and did not spare him.

At midday, Cael laid his mother's petition beneath the carrier's stone.

"Must I let the others read it?" he asked.

"No," Sef said. "The carrier decides whether a private record enters common custody. The witness may verify the seal without receiving the contents."

He showed Mara how to touch only the petition's edge. The stone produced date, authorship, and absence of alteration. Her face did not appear. Her words remained Cael's.

Cael's shoulders eased.

"The current Weave would take all of it," he said.

"The current Weave confuses completeness with honesty. A complete theft remains theft."

Sef's head swam. For an instant he could not recall whose petition lay before him. He recognized the boy, the linen, the maternal hand in the old script. The name would not come.

Cael noticed.

"Mine," he said. "Cael."

Sef held the name while it lasted. "Cael Verr. Carrier."

He cut the role beside the boy's hand mark.

Elian had once accused him of treating every tenderness as an entry to be indexed. Sef remembered the accusation but not the room in which it happened. He had laughed then. Or perhaps Elian had. The Threshold did not remove records evenly. It left enough to make every absence take shape.

Lin placed water beside him. "Rest."

"No."

"You taught closing. Use it."

Pride made him almost refuse. The old work had another rule: no reader interpreted while the body could no longer hold the angle. Sef accepted the cup.

They moved the stones to the floor and ate bread with pickled reed root. Mara remembered a meal in the outer settlement and described it without laying it on any stone. The Archivist listened. Cael asked whether food tasted different when attached to private memory.

"Every bowl carries the bowls before it," Mara said. "Sometimes that ruins supper. Sometimes it saves it."

Sef tasted onion in the root and found Elian absent again.

He resumed with a damaged reed map instead of the precious fragment. Training should fail on material that could survive a learner's error. Moisture had swollen one corner. Two route cuts crossed where later ink disguised a repair.

"Carry it to the window," he told Cael.

Cael lifted the map by its sound edge.

"Wrong."

The boy froze. "I did not touch the damage."

"You accepted destination from me without asking whether the source allowed movement. A carrier does not become a pair of obedient feet."

Sef asked the Archivist to state custody. The old man checked the outer cuts. This practice map belonged to no private line; its charter permitted teaching inside the alcove but prohibited removal into direct sun. Cael chose the low shelf near the window, where angled light reached the repair without heating the reeds.

"Again," Sef said.

Lin opened the map at the reader's angle. Mara described what she could verify: a marsh route, two inks, a repaired crossing, unknown date. The Archivist prepared to close if pressure split the swollen corner. Cael named the shelf as destination and reserved refusal.

Only then did they move it.

Sunlight disclosed pinholes along the later line. Lin interpreted them as an erased settlement boundary. Sef let her finish before he placed a black tile behind the reeds. The holes formed letters used by rope-makers to count seasonal depth.

"I made the boundary because I expected a map to hide one," Lin said.

"Expectation reads quickly," Sef replied. "Material reads at its own pace."

Mara knelt beside the shelf. Her inherited account placed a flooded road near that crossing, but the stable geometry ran farther east. She said both aloud. Lin marked disagreement with an open hook, then added possible causes: changed channel, copying drift, witness displacement, or a later route.

"Which one enters the map?" Cael asked.

"All until a source removes one," Lin said.

Sef heard modern training in the answer despite its improved shape. "A source does not remove uncertainty merely by arriving. It changes its weight."

He gave them four tiles, each cut with a different depth. Direct observation received the deepest. Verified contemporary copy came next. Inherited witness carried a variable edge. Interpretation remained shallow and had to sit visibly above whatever supported it.

Lin arranged the tiles beneath her hook. Her first placement gave the old reeds greater authority than Mara's account simply because they could be touched.

"Paper and stone also lie," Mara said.

"Reeds," Cael corrected.

"Reeds lie while lasting longer than a mouth."

Sef made Lin examine the repair. Someone had cut a route on old material with newer ink, then smoked the corner to age it. The route could still be accurate. False age did not prove false geography, but it changed the claim the object could support.

They built two witness copies. Lin's first smoothed the disputed crossing into a dotted line. Sef scraped it clean.

"You turned disagreement into weak certainty."

"A dotted line means uncertain in the Hall."

"Uncertain to whom? About what? Whether the road existed, where it ran, or when someone traveled it? One mark cannot carry every absence."

Her second copy separated existence, position, and date. Mara added her testimony only to position. The Archivist certified material alteration without guessing motive. Cael carried the finished copy to the table, then returned it to the shelf when the old reeds began curling.

Closing became urgent as the room warmed. The Archivist turned his stone. Nothing happened.

He tried again with more pressure.

"Stop," Sef said.

The old man lifted both hands. Embarrassment colored his neck. "I know this cut."

"You know the vault's plate. This closure follows water, not iron."

Sef could have corrected the angle himself. Instead he asked Mara what had changed since opening. She named sunlight, heat, curling reeds, altered carrier destination, and a second witness copy. Cael noticed condensation gathering beneath the black tile. Lin found the water route closing at the damp corner rather than the old mark.

The Archivist moved his palm there. The map released at once.

"A closer responds to present conditions," Sef said. "Rank cannot close by memory of the last device."

The lesson made the Archivist pale. Forty-three years at one door had taught him procedure so narrow it resembled mastery until the object changed.

Sef asked Cael to carry the map back to storage. This time the boy requested custody terms, named a dry destination, checked the route for sunlight, and asked whether another person should share the weight. The reeds weighed less than a loaf. Shared carrying still mattered because a stumble could force one hand to choose between body and source.

Iven, the Archive clerk, had returned with two slings used for damaged rolls. She waited beyond the curtain until Mara explained what witness access would include. Iven agreed to see the material condition without hearing Mara's inherited route. Privacy could be divided more finely than open or sealed.

Cael and Iven suspended the reed map between them. Halfway to the storage niche, Sef told them to halt.

"Why?" Cael asked.

"Because I said so."

The boy looked at the map, then at Iven. "No damage. No changed route. Reader gives no source. I decline the halt."

Sef felt satisfaction arrive with grief behind it. Elian had once refused him that plainly. He no longer held the occasion, only the shape of being corrected by someone he loved.

"Proceed," he said.

They set the reeds in the dry niche. Iven signed only for observed condition. Cael marked destination. Lin recorded her interpretation separately. Mara's account remained attached by consent and could be removed without falsifying the physical record. The Archivist closed custody after checking air on both sides of the shelf.

One minor map had required five corrections, two carriers, a renegotiated witness boundary, and an old man's failed command. No revelation rewarded them. The route still crossed at an uncertain point.

"This is too slow for the council," Lin said.

"Then the council must learn the cost of not pretending," Sef replied.

His dizziness returned as he gathered the depth tiles. He misplaced interpretation beneath direct observation and did not notice. Mara caught the error before Lin copied it.

Sef stared at his own hands. Knowledge could survive in a method while failing inside its teacher. That had always been the reason for divided work. The late Threshold merely removed his privilege of ignoring it.

He asked each of them to state one error from the exercise under their own name. Lin named expectation. Mara named the temptation to make inherited geography answer every date. The Archivist named habitual closure. Cael named obedience mistaken for carrying. Iven named a signature offered without full access.

Sef named authority used without cause.

They cut those admissions into the practice copy's margin. Future readers would receive not a polished demonstration but the conditions under which five people had almost made it false.

After the meal, he brought out the last cartographer's fragment.

The stone strip had traveled through six Keepers. Its old script named the Verr woman who folded the square and hid the counter-map. It also carried instructions for a future return. Sef had spent forty years assembling the surrounding pieces.

Lin read the first line by touch.

"Reader opens no record alone," she translated.

Mara took the witness mark.

"Witness confirms source without owning experience."

Cael found the carrier's line.

"Carrier chooses the destination and may refuse."

The closing line belonged to Sef. He guided the Archivist's hand to it instead.

The old man flinched. "You said I do not keep today."

"Closing is not keeping. That confusion built your vault."

The Archivist read: "Closer prevents use without destroying custody."

Four people stood around the fragment. Four human bodies, four hands in contact, four roles no one person could complete. The stone opened.

The last cartographer appeared only as an impression of work: a quick copy made while the Suppression moved through the square, the old border hidden beneath the revised one, a route from the Underloom seam to the pre-Silence vault. Along the margin she had cut a warning.

If these hands become one office, close the map until they separate again.

Lin looked toward the Archivist.

He kept his palm on the closer's mark. "They became one office. Mine."

"Not yours alone," Sef said. "The Hall consolidated reading. The Weaver-Prime controlled opening. The Archive claimed custody. Each pretended the others preserved the balance. None could be refused by a citizen."

"How do we separate them now?" Lin asked.

"Not by naming heroes. Build offices that can say no to you."

The answer disappointed her. Sef saw it. She wanted a tool, perhaps a Cartography of Return whose elegance would solve the moral problem. Craft could route memory. It could not manufacture consent.

"Your map will fail if the citizen has no hand on it," he said.

"A citizen cannot read the Underloom."

"Then you teach enough that refusal has meaning."

Mara turned the witness stone beneath her palm. "The first returned memory should not be the Suppression."

"No."

"A face," Cael said. "One person. Chosen by people who knew them."

Sef looked at the boy. Cael's unSilenced mind had grasped the scale better than the adults. A people trained to yield every private yesterday could not consent to an entire buried war. They might consent to hold one face for one night.

Lin drew four columns on barkcloth: selection, witness, closing, carrying. Under selection she wrote citizen chooses. Under witness, source disclosed. Under closing, refusal leaves record intact. Under carrying, memory remains with recipient only by consent.

"That is not a map yet," she said.

"Good," Sef replied. "Maps arrive after duties."

The next loss came while he watched her write.

He forgot the word for her profession.

He knew the hand, the red hem, the trained attention. He knew the guild had once belonged to borders rather than obedience. The noun vanished. He pointed to her.

"Say what you are."

Lin understood.

"Cartographer."

"Again."

"Cartographer."

Sef repeated it until the shape returned. "One who draws the conditions of crossing. Not one who tells the world where it ends."

She added the definition to the map.

Evening gathered outside the alcove. The patrol bell sounded, then the market bell, then the low closing note from the Hall. Each arrived with fewer associations than it had that morning.

Sef wrapped the last cartographer's fragment and gave it to Lin.

"No," she said again.

"You refuse gifts badly."

"It belongs here."

"It belongs where it can be used under the old division. The alcove dies with the Keeper if the work stays attached to my body."

"Then come to the council."

He touched her cheek. The gesture surprised them both. He remembered making it to another young person once. Daughter, student, sister's child. The relationship had gone, leaving tenderness without a name.

"I will speak if I can," he said. "If I cannot, the stone speaks with my source attached. That is why we make records. Not to replace memory. To keep one person's loss from ending everyone else's access."

Lin accepted the fragment.

Before they left, Sef asked them to perform the sequence without him. Lin opened. Mara witnessed. The Archivist closed. Cael chose to carry the copied border to the council. No role overrode another. When Cael asked to exclude his mother's petition, the map closed around that absence without treating it as an error.

Sef watched the four hands withdraw.

The architecture held.

After the others went, the Archivist remained at the curtain.

"Elian," he said.

Sef looked up.

"Your wife's name. You asked me years ago to keep it if you lost the smell of onions."

The name opened no face. It did open a warmth behind his ribs, grief fitted to an absence.

"Did I ask you to tell me anything else?"

"You said not to describe her. You said a description made by me would become a counterfeit memory. Only the name."

Sef nodded. His earlier self had understood the boundary.

He cut Elian into a private stone and closed it with his own hand. No witness. No carrier. Some records did not belong to the Hold.

On the public table, the four custodial marks remained around an empty center. In the street, Lin and the others carried the method toward the council.

Sef listened to their footsteps fade. He could no longer remember who had eaten bread in the alcove that afternoon. He could still read the arrangement they left behind.

Reader. Witness. Closer. Carrier.

No hand alone.

Chapter 15 — A Council Divided

Orrin Tel opened the council hearing by placing his oath on the table.

The object was a plain black ring worn by every Weaver-Prime since the First Weaving. Its inner edge carried the words keep the Hold safe. Tradition supplied the rest: preserve the peace, guard the Weave, maintain the Silence. The ring itself said none of those things.

Around the circular chamber, nine councilors watched him remove it.

"This is not a surrender," Orrin said. "It is evidence. We have treated an interpretation as if the metal carried it. Today we separate what the oath says from what we taught one another it must mean."

Corrin sat at the senior Cartographer's place with both hands flat on the stone. Lin stood behind the witness rail beside Mara Sol, Cael Verr, Sef, and the Archivist without his cord. Councilor Pell occupied the water seat, a narrow man with a reed-worker's scar across his chin. He had read Lin's first day map and questioned her black line before anyone else admitted seeing it.

The chamber doors closed.

Orrin asked the clerk to record every speaker's source and interest before argument. It was an old procedure recovered from Sef's fragment, and Corrin objected immediately.

"A councilor's rank establishes standing," he said.

"Rank establishes power," Sef replied from the witness rail. "Standing tells us where the power learned its facts."

Orrin sustained the new procedure.

The Archivist testified first. He named the pre-Silence surveys, their custody, and his own decision to keep them locked for forty-three years. He did not use preservation as a defense. When Councilor Pell asked whether any source had been altered since the Suppression, the Archivist described the seals, material, and corroboration chain. Mara's inherited testimony had not touched the maps before the vault opened. The Underloom's folded comparison had no access to the paper. All three agreed at the disputed line.

The clerk displayed copies on the central table.

Black ink showed two towns sharing a seasonal channel. Red ink showed the House's later revisions, each granting the same land to a different town. The room leaned toward the map despite itself.

"Manufactured grievance," Pell said.

Corrin's jaw tightened. "A forged survey does not prove the entire war was manufactured."

Lin answered from the rail. "No single item proves that claim. The tax orders, matched petitions, House correspondence, and folded witness cluster establish a chain. The map proves the disputed line was knowingly falsified."

"You entered the Underloom without authority."

"Yes."

The unqualified admission disturbed the council more than an excuse might have.

Orrin asked Cael to present the clean-up registry. The boy carried the copy to the table but kept his mother's private petition beneath his arm.

"Why is that document excluded?" Corrin asked.

"Because it belongs to me," Cael said. "Its seal and date are verified. Its private words are not required to establish the registry."

"A witness cannot withhold evidence at pleasure."

Mara stepped forward. "A system that requires surrender of every private word before it recognizes harm has learned nothing from the harm."

Corrin looked to Orrin. "Will you permit an outsider to instruct this council?"

"I will permit the witness whose memory our loom verified to define the boundary of her testimony."

That marked the first division.

Councilor Bren, keeper of grain, moved that the red room be sealed under joint guard until a larger review could determine authenticity. Corrin supported him. Two others joined at once. Their argument did not deny the evidence. It emphasized consequence: the Hold's peace had survived three generations; old grievances, once returned, could reattach to living families; the Weave's stability depended on citizens yielding rather than holding contradictory private histories.

"We have no measure for mass refusal," Corrin said. "No model for ten thousand minds retaining burdens the substrate expects to carry. The fold may be a crime. Opening it without understanding load could become a massacre performed in truth's name."

Orrin wrote the objection down. Lin did too.

Mara's expression sharpened. "He is not wrong about the load."

Corrin seemed more angered by her agreement than by opposition.

Councilor Pell moved that the registry and forged maps be published immediately, with the fold opened to any citizen who wished to read. Two younger councilors supported him. Their faction treated delay as continuation of the Suppression.

"Every hour the council keeps this private repeats the act," Pell said. "The citizens cannot decide what they are not told exists."

"If you open the whole fold," Mara replied, "you deny them decision by overwhelming it."

"Then who decides the rate? You? The Verr? Another guild office?"

"No single hand," Sef said.

He explained the old division: reader, witness, closer, carrier. Four ordinary people, four authorities. A reader could open but not transport. A witness could verify without owning the private contents. A closer could halt use without erasure. A carrier chose destination and retained the right to refuse.

Corrin tapped the table. "A ritual from before the Weave cannot govern the Weave."

"It can expose what the current guild removed," Lin said. "Independent refusal."

Orrin asked her to state the staged proposal.

She unrolled a preliminary map. It contained no triumphant route from silence to memory. It began with questions.

First: Can one chosen citizen receive one low-conflict private memory without destabilizing the substrate?

Second: Can the citizen close access afterward while the source remains preserved?

Third: Can seven unrelated citizens hold the same verified face without forced synchronization?

Fourth: Can the Hold publicly acknowledge the existence of the fold before receiving its contested history?

Only after those tests would the rebellion's record surface, paired with the forged maps and the guild's own objections.

"You ask us to experiment on citizens," Bren said.

"The Threshold already does," Cael replied.

Silence entered the chamber.

The boy continued. "It experimented on me and failed. The Archive hid the failure because the Hall would have classified me as danger. If your peace cannot survive one fourteen-year-old remembering his mother, it isn't safety. It's dependence."

Orrin watched Corrin. The senior Cartographer looked at Cael without contempt. Fear, not cruelty, governed him. That made his faction harder to dismiss.

"What do you remember at this moment?" Corrin asked.

"Your left hand moved when I said mother. You remember something your mind doesn't."

Corrin withdrew the hand.

"That is precisely the danger," he said. "Private inference without common verification."

"Then verify it," Cael said. "Don't remove it."

The second division hardened.

The permanent-silence faction wanted the vault secured and the Underloom resealed. The immediate-return faction wanted public release before the guild could bury the evidence again. Between them, Orrin found himself beside Lin's staged-consent proposal, not because it promised safety but because it preserved the citizen's ability to stop.

He had not yet declared that position.

The council recessed at midday. No one left the chamber. Food arrived on covered trays. The witnesses ate behind the rail while councilors formed clusters.

Pell approached Orrin. "If you support stages, Corrin will use every stage as a reason to delay the next."

"If I support immediate release, how many citizens wake tomorrow carrying the square?"

"All of them, if the Weave accepts it."

"That is not consent."

Pell looked toward the copies. "Nor is ignorance."

Both statements held.

The clerk interrupted the private arguments with three sealed packets from the wards. News of the Archive's redistributed linen had traveled faster than formal testimony. Public-shelf readers wanted to know why damaged rolls had gone unwrapped while a concealed room received five bolts at each delivery. Dock porters demanded protection from charges if their invoices became evidence. Twenty-one Archive clerks had signed a request to testify about classifications they could record but never inspect.

Corrin reached for the packets. Orrin stopped him.

"Source and interest first."

Each petition named its makers, their work, and the consequence they feared. The readers feared loss of civic records. Porters feared punishment for following concealed orders. Clerks feared the council would blame minor hands while preserving senior offices. None asked yet for returned memory. Their concerns involved budgets, labor, and exposure.

Pell read the porter petition twice. "They should be admitted before the vote."

Bren objected that the hearing concerned substrate safety, not Archive accounts.

"Concealment used those accounts," Orrin said. "Procedure belongs to the harm."

They opened the upper gallery. Seven petitioners entered because the chamber could not hold all signers. Iven Sal spoke for the clerks. Salt stains remained on her cuffs from moving damaged rolls that morning.

"We signed preservation totals," she said. "Our marks were presented as oversight. We could see quantity but not destination. If you call us witnesses now, record that our access did not match the word."

Councilor Jori, who had not joined a faction, asked whether any clerk had suspected the hidden allocation.

"We suspected favoritism," Iven replied. "A senior collection kept dry while ordinary rolls failed. We did not know its contents. Suspicion without a route to inspect becomes corridor talk. Corridor talk can cost a clerk's place."

Corrin questioned whether restoring four custodial roles would have changed that. Iven looked toward the four palm cuts on Sef's copied charter.

"Not if senior officers choose all four people. Not if refusal ends wages. Not if witness means signing a total through a closed door."

Her answer troubled every proposal. Permanent silence required staff to maintain sealed evidence. Immediate return required workers to operate looms under orders they might reject. Staged consent required an independent closer who could lose employment by stopping a trial.

Orrin asked each faction who would pay a closer during a disputed halt.

Corrin proposed the Hall, subject to discipline for negligent interruption.

Pell proposed the wards, but could not name how poor wards would support trained officers through a long closure.

Lin requested a common fund set beyond guild budgets, with removal decided by the citizens' assembly rather than the reader's office.

Bren laughed without humor. "You designed a new institution overnight and now want grain money for it."

"The vault already consumed public money," Iven said. "It merely did so without a line you had to defend."

That shifted Jori toward staged consent. It also moved one immediate-return councilor back to abstention. Public machinery made the middle proposal more credible and more expensive at once.

A dock porter named Ulen presented the morning invoice. Five linen bolts had entered the Archive category for nineteen consecutive years. He had carried deliveries for six of them.

"If those receipts become proof of concealment, am I entered in the registry?" he asked.

"As source," Lin said.

"As accused?"

"No."

Pell leaned forward. "Citizens may judge complicity differently."

Orrin saw Ulen's knuckles whiten around the paper. Immediate publication without a distinction between administrative trace and responsible choice could turn every low worker into a target. Permanent sealing would preserve the same workers' fear beneath official quiet.

He ordered the clerk to create separate marks for action, authority, access, and known consequence. A name could occupy more than one field. No chain would collapse into a list of guilty hands.

Corrin objected that such distinctions invited excuse.

"They also locate command," Orrin said.

Bren's authorization appeared on six recent allocations. The grain keeper studied the copied entries, then stated that he had approved an Archive total without destinations because custom did not require them.

"Did you know a red room existed?" Pell asked.

"No."

"Did you ask why preservation costs rose while public losses increased?"

Bren pressed his lips together. "No."

His own faction received the admission poorly. Seal advocates wanted caution directed toward future risk, not scrutiny of the habits that had maintained past concealment.

The gallery petitioners stayed while each faction amended its stopping condition. Corrin allowed that any reseal must publish costs and assign a ward witness to the vault exterior, though he would not permit source access. Pell accepted a one-bell warning before release, enough for citizens to leave public loom rooms. Lin added wage protection for closers, witnesses, and carriers who halted work.

None satisfied Iven.

"A ward witness outside a locked room still sees a door," she told Corrin. "One bell does not let a well keeper abandon pumps," she told Pell. To Lin she said, "Protection written by a tied council can vanish with its next vote. Where does appeal go?"

Lin had no answer ready. She wrote an open hook on her proposal.

Orrin felt the hearing change scale. They had begun by debating whether citizens could bear memory. Workers now asked whether institutions could bear refusal without punishing the person who spoke it.

Before dismissing the gallery, he required the petitioners to choose what entered the common record. Ulen released the invoice but withheld his home ward. Iven made her full statement public and kept the names of junior clerks sealed unless each consented. Two public-shelf readers allowed images of rot but refused removal of the damaged rolls from their care.

The clerk recorded each boundary. Council time lengthened. No one called the delay waste while citizens sat above them watching.

A public-shelf reader raised one last complication. The damaged rolls could not remain in the gallery, yet returning them before the hearing ended would remove visible evidence of the allocation's cost. He asked for a guard who knew preservation and carried no guild rank.

Bren offered a grain clerk. Pell objected that the choice came from a councilor implicated in the accounts. Iven nominated the porter who had refused vault entry that morning. His refusal had cost him half a delivery wage and demonstrated no allegiance to disclosure.

Orrin ordered compensation from the concealed maintenance line. The porter accepted custody after inspecting the rolls, the gallery exit, and the clerk's written limit. He would protect material condition, not prevent readers from viewing damage. Council authority could not move the rolls without his mark until adjournment.

One narrow refusal became paid civic work before any faction won its larger argument. Orrin entered that consequence beside the petitions.

Across the chamber, Corrin spoke quietly with Bren. Orrin caught only fragments: loom load, ward failures, emergency seal. The senior Cartographer had already begun planning resistance if the vote went against him.

Lin approached the Prime's place but remained outside its marked circle.

"The live thread can carry a narrow memory upward," she said. "I have mapped the route. I have not tested the return."

"Could you open the whole fold?"

"Possibly."

"Would you?"

"No."

Orrin studied her. "Because the council has not authorized it?"

"Because the people have not."

That answer moved him farther than the evidence had.

When the hearing resumed, Orrin required each faction to answer the same three questions: what harm does your plan risk, who may refuse it, and how can the decision be reversed?

Corrin answered first. Permanent silence risked continuing a historical crime and denying citizens knowledge needed for consent. The council could refuse on their behalf under the existing charter. Reversal remained possible because the fold survived.

Pell answered next. Immediate return risked grief, unrest, and substrate instability. Individual refusal would be offered after public opening. Reversal would be impossible once private memory entered citizen minds.

Lin answered for staged consent. The trials risked harm to a small number of volunteers and could normalize guild control if the stages never passed to public authority. Every carrier could refuse before and during contact. The map could close after each trial without destroying the source.

"Who decides when the Hold is ready?" Bren asked.

"Not the Hall alone," Lin said. "A citizens' witness assembly, chosen from wards, with authority to stop us."

"Us?" Corrin said.

"Anyone who can read the route. Including me."

The third division formed around that admission. Two councilors who had favored immediate return shifted toward stages if citizens could control the pace. One from Corrin's bloc asked whether the fold could remain sealed while the assembly learned enough to make a meaningful decision.

No one changed sides cleanly.

The vote came at dusk.

On Bren's motion to reseal the stair permanently: three for, five against, one abstention.

On Pell's motion for immediate public return: three for, four against, two abstentions.

On Lin's proposal for a seven-person voluntary trial under divided custody: four for, four against.

Orrin held the deciding seat.

Every face turned toward him.

The oath ring lay on the table under his hand. Keep the Hold safe. For twenty-two years he had treated safety and silence as interchangeable. The evidence had separated them. It had not told him which danger carried the smaller cost.

"I do not vote tonight," he said.

Pell stood. "Prime, the charter requires you to break a tie."

"The charter requires the council to hear a matter before decision. We heard sources. We have not heard the people who will carry the result. I order one night for each faction to name its proposed citizen authority and its stopping condition. We reconvene tomorrow."

Corrin rose more slowly. "Delay favors the status quo."

"Then use the night to propose a status quo the citizens may refuse."

"There is no such mechanism."

"That is the defect before us."

Orrin struck the closing stone.

The council divided into three groups before the sound faded. Corrin left by the west door with Bren and the seal faction. Pell took the east door with the immediate-return councilors. Lin and the witnesses remained by the central table, neither a guild faction nor yet a public body.

Sef sagged against the rail. The Archivist caught his elbow. Cael gathered the copied registry. Mara rolled the forged maps separately so no one packet could remove both.

Lin looked at Orrin. "Tomorrow you must choose."

"Yes."

"Corrin will not wait."

Orrin looked toward the west door. The senior Cartographer possessed authority over the Hall's cutters and emergency seals. By dawn, the stair might be guarded or closed.

"Neither will Pell," he said.

The immediate-return faction could reach the public looms through the water records. Each side might force the outcome it feared the other would choose.

Orrin put the oath ring back on his finger. The words had not changed. Their meaning no longer arrived ready-made.

He left by the southern passage toward the third well, alone, while behind him the council chamber held three abandoned proposals and no peace that could pretend it had not been chosen.

Chapter 16 — Mara Teaches

Mara rejected the council's first list of volunteers because it contained seven people who had not volunteered.

Each name carried an official reason: useful anomaly, half-remembered capacity, stable work history, no dependent children. The council had selected bodies the way the Hall selected instruments.

She tore the list down the middle.

"Bring me people who heard the risk and came anyway."

Pell frowned across Sef's table. "If we announce the lesson, Corrin's faction will call it sedition."

"Then let them call it by its name in public. Secrecy already has a guild."

By evening, eleven citizens stood outside the alcove. Mara explained the trial on the street because no room should hide the terms. They would receive or summon one small memory. The Weave might pull at it. Holding could cause pain, disorientation, grief, shared dreams, or unknown harm. Any participant could stop. Refusal would not become evidence of incapacity.

Four left.

The seven who remained did not resemble a chosen vanguard.

Bereth, a market weaver, remembered textures after losing names. Telma Rusk made rope in the western ward and wanted to know why the western gate frightened her. Darel kept wells and retained songs but not singers. Osa was a baker whose private memories vanished normally, except for the smell of smoke. Kimn copied tax rolls and distrusted Mara's account. Vel had been Silenced late after illness and lost pieces unpredictably. Neri, the youngest at thirteen, had passed the Threshold three months earlier and woke each day angry without knowing at whom.

Cael joined as demonstrator, not volunteer. Mara wrote the distinction on the wall.

"He is not the standard," she told them. "His Silence failed. Your minds learned yielding for years. Do not measure your first hour against his whole life."

She began without the Weave.

Each person chose an object from a covered basket: button, reed ring, smooth stone, spoon, red cord, seed, shell. They studied it for thirty breaths. Then Mara covered the objects and asked them to describe only what they chose to keep.

Osa listed everything and remembered nothing ten minutes later. Bereth retained the roughness of the cord but gave its color incorrectly. Neri refused to speak. Kimn accused Mara of changing the basket.

"Good," she said.

Kimn looked offended. "I failed."

"You disputed the source. That belongs in memory work."

She uncovered the objects. They compared description with fact. Mara marked uncertainty without shame. Holding did not mean claiming perfect recall. Her inherited Suppression contained dry stones and remembered rain; honesty required both versions.

Next she asked them to choose a memory already at their edges.

Bereth found her mother's hand guiding a shuttle. Darel chose four notes from a boat song. Osa located smoke but refused the room around it. Telma had no face, only the western gate and a bodily need to turn away. Kimn chose that morning's tax column to test a fact he could verify. Vel found a brother's coat without the brother. Neri said she possessed nothing.

Cael hummed three falling notes and held the fourth.

"What do you do when the Weave pulls?" Darel asked.

"It doesn't feel like pulling to me," Cael said. "It feels like everyone expecting me to put something down."

The adults looked at him.

"That matters," Mara said. "The system trained yielding until pressure feels social before it feels mechanical. We practice noticing both."

She taught them a boundary phrase: mine to keep, mine to share, mine to close. Not an incantation with power over the substrate. Language merely gave the mind a sequence when panic scattered attention.

They placed palms on Sef's lay-stone one at a time. Mara served as witness. Sef held the closing angle. Lin read only the route and did not enter the memory.

Bereth went first.

The mother's hand appeared on the shuttle, broad thumb, split nail, blue thread caught in the cuff. The Weave warmed and invited the image outward. Bereth tightened every muscle. The memory distorted. Her mother's hand grew enormous, the shuttle narrowing beneath it.

"Stop fighting the picture," Mara said. "Hold the boundary, not every detail."

"If I loosen, it goes."

"If you grip detail, fear redraws it. Choose what matters."

Bereth breathed. "The thumb. The blue thread. How she corrected me without taking the shuttle."

The image settled.

After twenty breaths, she asked Sef to close. He turned the stone. Bereth removed her hand and vomited into the basin.

Pell moved toward her. Mara blocked him until Bereth nodded permission.

"Still worth it?" he asked.

Bereth wiped her mouth. "Ask tomorrow."

That became the first rule added by a carrier: no verdict demanded immediately after contact.

Darel held his song but lost the singer. Osa touched the smoke and screamed before the room formed. Sef closed at once. She refused a second attempt and sat outside until the shaking passed.

"The memory injured her," Kimn said.

"The edge did," Mara replied. "We do not yet know whether the room contains harm, warning, or both. Her refusal stays closed."

"Then what did the lesson prove?"

"That stopping works."

Kimn's trial retained a tax column perfectly. He emerged triumphant until Lin showed him the source sheet: he had transposed two figures before contact and preserved the mistake.

"Memory can hold a false entry," she said. "Persistence does not prove truth."

Kimn's triumph became attention.

Vel's brother's coat opened into a winter road. He wept at the cuff and asked for more before the first contact ended. Mara refused.

"I consent."

"You consent while flooded. The agreed duration remains twenty breaths. You may ask again tomorrow."

"That is my brother."

"Which is why grief does not get to renegotiate alone."

He cursed her when Sef closed. Later he thanked Sef, not Mara. She accepted the distinction.

Neri would not touch the stone.

"Everyone says I have an anger," the girl said. "Maybe I don't. Maybe they want an explanation because anger without a story is inconvenient."

"Then do not take one from us."

Neri sat through every lesson as observer. Her refusal taught more about choice than another successful hold could have.

Telma approached last. The western gate surfaced under fog. No person appeared. Her body folded inward as if expecting a blow.

"Close," she whispered.

Sef turned the angle. The stone did not respond.

The Weave had caught the memory's edge and continued pulling.

Lin reduced reader pressure. Mara kept Telma's gaze.

"Name the room," Mara said.

"No room. Gate. Mud. A hand letting go."

"Name yourself."

"Telma Rusk. Rope-maker. Western ward."

"What do you choose?"

"Close the hand. Keep the gate."

Sef shifted to the older closing mark. The route released.

Telma fell against Mara. The gate remained as fact. The touch vanished behind a boundary Telma chose.

The failed closure changed the entire trial. A carrier's word did not guarantee the substrate would obey. Choice required a person trained and equipped to enforce it. They added independent closer, tested mechanism, and immediate stop conditions to Lin's draft.

At sunset, the seven left with written cards stating what each had chosen and what remained uncertain. Bereth's card named blue thread and correction without seizure. Darel's named four notes, singer unknown. Osa's named smoke, room closed. Kimn's named a transposed figure to remind him persistence could preserve error. Vel's named coat only. Neri's read no contact chosen. Telma's named western gate, hand closed.

Cael carried no card. Mara gave him one anyway.

"Why?"

"Because natural retention does not make your memory public."

He wrote his mother's song and folded the card into his pocket.

The next morning, five returned. Osa sent word that she would not. Vel arrived before dawn and waited without demanding the next memory. Bereth brought the shuttle from her workbench and compared its motion with what she had held.

The blue thread remained.

"Worth it?" Pell asked, remembering the delay.

Bereth considered. "Ask in a week."

They practiced without contact that day. Mara taught source checks, grounding, and the difference between holding and forcing. Cael described how one private memory could sit beside another without merging. Kimn challenged him with dates. Neri noticed that adult participants praised Cael every time he recalled correctly and warned them to stop turning the boy into proof.

Mara wrote that on the wall too.

Their first source-check exercise used Bereth's shuttle. She placed it inside a cloth sleeve before anyone else entered and gave Mara permission to reveal only the movement of its weight, not its color or maker's cuts. Kimn served as observer. Darel held the closing card. Neri chose to time the exercise without touching either object or stone.

Bereth repeated the remembered correction. Her thumb pressed lightly behind the spindle while two fingers loosened. The real shuttle moved farther than memory predicted and struck the table.

She recoiled. "Then I kept it wrong."

"You kept a gesture from childhood," Mara said. "Your hand has grown. The shuttle may differ."

Lin inspected the wood after Bereth allowed it. A replacement peg altered the balance. The remembered motion could be accurate for an earlier tool, inaccurate for this object, or partly redrawn by fear.

Kimn wanted a verdict.

"Which claim needs one?" Mara asked.

"Whether her mother corrected her that way."

"No object can settle the hand. It can test what the hand did to a tool."

Bereth requested another attempt with the current shuttle. Mara refused immediate repetition. Frustration tightened Bereth's mouth, but she wrote the change on her card: movement retained; present tool disagrees; old peg unknown. Her mother's tenderness did not depend on mechanical precision. Public teaching about weaving might.

Darel's song created a different problem. He remembered four notes overnight, yet Cael heard the third rise where Darel insisted it fell. The lay-stone showed no source recording. Darel accused Cael of carrying a variant from another ward.

"I might," Cael said. "That doesn't make yours false."

"Two versions cannot lead the same hauling rhythm."

Mara asked them to tap each version against rope work. Telma provided a short coil. Darel's descending phrase matched the pull used at eastern boats. Cael's rising third matched no pull Telma knew, though Neri recognized it from a market game.

The memory had crossed contexts, perhaps through Cael's retained days or common sound in the Weave. No one could identify when. They preserved both phrases with separate uses rather than forcing a single original.

"A song may have cousins," Neri said. "Adults do."

Darel laughed, then stopped when laughter brought a singer's missing mouth to the edge of recall. His fingers dug into the rope.

Mara saw the flood before he named it. She raised one open hand. Darel chose closure. Sef held no stone that afternoon; the group needed to prove its stopping practice did not depend entirely on him. Vel served as closer under Lin's direction.

He turned too far.

The route pinched rather than released. Darel gasped and began singing both versions at once. Mara pulled Vel's wrist away before panic made him force the angle.

"I failed him," Vel said.

"You stopped when corrected. Stay present."

Lin took no closer authority because she remained reader. She told Vel where the route held but left the next motion to him. He reset his palm, asked Darel whether he still wanted closure, and waited for a nod. One shallow turn released the song.

Darel sat on the floor until his breath slowed. He did not thank Vel. He asked Neri to write the sequence precisely: wrong turn, reader warning, physical interruption, renewed consent, second closure.

"Include that Mara touched him without asking," he said.

Vel looked startled.

"She touched me," Darel said. "It prevented more harm. I still want the line recorded."

Mara felt heat move into her face. She had acted to stop a closer from forcing contact, not to control Vel's body. Purpose did not remove the contact.

"Write it," she told Neri.

They added an emergency rule: a witness could interrupt imminent mechanical harm, but the contact entered review and could be challenged by the person touched. No role received invisible exceptions for good intention.

Telma declined all stone work that day. Instead she mapped the alcove exits with rope and tested how quickly someone could leave after closure. The curtain snagged her shoulder. A water jar narrowed the western passage. Two observers had seated themselves between participants and the street.

"You say anyone can stop," she told Mara. "Can they get out without asking six knees to move?"

They rearranged the room. Chairs faced neither the lay-stone nor the door, leaving choice of attention. Basins moved within reach but outside the walking line. One clear path remained to the street. Neri marked it with chalk, then erased the mark after Osa's brother arrived demanding to know why she had refused to return.

He stood in the doorway, blocking the path they had just protected.

"She wakes screaming smoke," he said. "You opened something and sent her home with it."

Mara asked him to step aside. He refused until Pell moved toward him. That threat made the man's stance harder.

Neri slipped behind the table and opened the rear cloth panel Sef used in summer. Cool air entered. A second exit existed, hidden by habit rather than lock.

"No one corners a participant," Mara said. "Not family, council, or teacher. Move from the door."

Osa's brother moved. Grief had brought him; entitlement kept him there. Both needed names.

He wanted Osa's card. Mara would not show it. He wanted assurance the smoke meant no danger to their household. Nobody could provide one. He asked for Osa's memory to be reopened without her present so the family could learn what happened.

"No," Bereth said before Mara answered.

The refusal came from another carrier, not an outsider defending a rule. Others joined. Even Kimn, who distrusted Mara, stated that source verification did not create family ownership.

They offered aftercare instead: a quiet room, food delivered without questions, a closer available if the memory resurfaced, and a written route for Osa to return or permanently withdraw. Her brother accepted the food task. He left without the card.

Afterward, Mara asked the group what coercion had entered the room. Pell named his movement as threat. Bereth named collective pressure when everyone answered at once. Neri named the blocked door. Vel named praise offered to those who returned while Osa's absence risked looking like weakness. Kimn named the teacher's power to decide when repetition counted as flooded consent.

Mara wrote her own name beside that last risk.

They created paired reviews. A participant who wanted immediate expansion would speak first to someone with no control over the loom. That person could not approve contact, only help state the request after the flood passed. Approval would wait until the next waking period and require the carrier's renewed words.

Vel objected. "You still delay what belongs to me."

"Yes," Mara said. "The procedure may be too strict. Record your objection."

He did, pressing hard enough to tear the card. Instead of replacing it, he stitched the tear with blue thread from Bereth. Damage remained legible.

At dusk, Cael forgot one verse of his mother's song. Everyone watched him with the alarm they had promised not to turn into expectation.

"I forget things," he said. "Remembering is not permanent possession."

Mara asked whether he wanted help recovering it. He did not. The missing verse received no exercise, no concerned circle, no claim that his unusual mind had begun failing. He went to fetch supper and returned arguing about the size of the bread portion.

Ordinary irritation steadied the room more than another successful hold.

By the fourth day, Darel could keep his four notes overnight. Kimn retained the corrected tax entry after deliberately releasing the false one. Bereth held the gesture of a hand without claiming a complete face. Telma chose the gate again and kept the fog for ten breaths.

Vel did not receive more of the winter road. He spent the week learning to close the coat voluntarily. Wanting expansion did not make restraint failure.

Corrin came on the sixth day.

He stood outside the circle and watched Bereth complete a contact. "You are training unsynchronized private recall."

"We are training voluntary boundaries," Mara said.

"A memory held by one citizen cannot be verified by the common record."

Kimn answered him. "Neither could my wrong tax figure. Holding preserved it until Lin checked the source. The lesson includes correction."

Corrin looked at the transposition on Kimn's card. The argument reached him because it did not claim memory's purity.

"What happens when a carrier refuses correction?" he asked.

"Public decisions require shared evidence," Mara said. "Private meaning does not require guild agreement. We separate the two."

"And when private meaning produces public violence?"

"Then judge the act. Do not remove every private life in advance."

He stayed through two more trials.

Orrin arrived at the end of the week. He asked whether the lesson proved the Hold could carry the Suppression.

"No," Mara said. "It proves five people held small boundaries under supervision. One refused. One left. One closure failed before the older mark worked. Scale increases risk. Grief changes consent. The only honest proof is narrow."

"Then what use is it to the council?"

"It tells them memory can survive and stopping can work. It also tells them where the first map was naive."

Lin unrolled the revised conditions. The teaching had added delayed consent review, source comparison, non-contact participation, an independent closer, and protection against making Cael the model for Silenced minds.

The seven had not become an argument for return. They had become authors of its limits.

On the seventh night, Bereth slept with the blue thread in memory. At dawn she returned to the alcove carrying the real shuttle. Her thumb found the place her mother's hand had corrected without seizing the tool.

She completed the movement alone.

Mara watched craft pass through memory without becoming grievance. Around them, the other cards rested on the wall: partial, corrected, delayed, refused.

No single word described the trial.

That was its strongest result.

Chapter 17 — The Map Of Return

Lin tore up the first Cartography of Return because its opening sentence contained the word give.

She had written: The guild will give the buried memory back to the Hold.

Mara read the line over her shoulder and set one finger beneath give.

"You still think the memory belongs to the person controlling the route," she said.

Lin looked at the torn sheets around the Keeper's stone. "The guild controls the loom."

"That is a description of power, not ownership."

The correction cost Lin six hours of work.

They drafted in Sef's alcove because the Hall's upper stones could not represent voluntary closure. Every guild map assumed that an open route should remain open until its ranked operator ended the reading. The old lay-stone permitted the carrier to stop.

On one wall, Lin pinned the Lost Cartography's twelve categories. On another, she placed the vault's provenance chain. A third held the council's objections: substrate load, revived grievance, coercive release, permanent delay, guild capture. She left the fourth wall empty for harms they had not anticipated.

A true return map had to include routes away from return.

Cael sat at the carrier's corner with his mother's song closed inside him. Mara held witness. The Archivist occupied the closer's place. Lin read the live seam through Sef's stone.

Sef himself slept behind the curtain. The late Threshold had taken three names that morning. When awake, he could still correct their method. When asleep, his hand twitched through the old opening angle.

Lin began with the substrate.

The live thread linked the folded Underloom to the accessible Weave. It could carry a narrow memory upward because the Suppression had left it outside the deepest compression. It could not bear unrestricted load. When Lin tested the topology with no contents attached, the seam widened at three pressure points and strained at a fourth near the Hall.

She marked each bottleneck.

"How narrow?" Cael asked.

"One sensory memory can pass cleanly. A face produces flinch. An event cluster would force the upper Weave to synchronize every citizen attached at that hour."

"Then no event cluster," Mara said.

"Not initially."

"Not unless citizens ask for one."

Lin amended the line.

The first stage would offer seven volunteers a domestic memory chosen by its source. No contested history. No anonymous extraction. The source would remain present. The carrier could stop contact at any moment. The closer would terminate the route without deleting the memory or recording the refusal as pathology.

"Why seven?" the Archivist asked.

"Enough to detect whether the Weave forces synchronization across unrelated threads. Few enough to observe each person."

"And if one becomes ill afterward?"

"The trial closes."

"For how long?"

Lin stopped.

The modern guild used indefinite review to turn temporary caution into burial. The old rebellion had asked for a reconsideration date. She wrote: Seven days, then the citizens' witness assembly decides whether to resume.

"Who chooses the assembly?" Cael asked.

"The council proposed ward lots."

"The council proposed itself choosing the eligible names."

Lin crossed that out. Each ward would nominate three citizens; random draw would choose one. No ranked Weaver, Cartographer, Archivist, councilor, or member of their household could serve in the first assembly.

The Archivist smiled without amusement. "You exclude every person trained to understand the instruments."

"We advise. They stop."

"Then teach them enough to know when."

That became a stage before the first stage: public instruction on the fold, the live thread, and the four custodial roles. Not four symbolic palms on a sacred stone. Four authorities made legible enough that a citizen could recognize concentration of power.

Reader opens.

Witness verifies source.

Closer halts access without erasing custody.

Carrier chooses whether and where memory travels.

No person holds two roles in the same reading.

Mara objected to the last line. "A source may also carry her own memory. I did."

"Then she holds carrier and source, not two custodial authorities."

"Write that distinction."

Lin did.

By midday the map had grown to thirty-two clauses and no path beyond the domestic trial. Lin wanted to draw the whole route before presenting it. Cartographic training mistrusted incomplete edges.

Sef woke and read the sheets from his bed.

"You are drawing permission as a corridor," he said.

"A staged route requires sequence."

"Sequence, yes. Destination, no. If the first seven choose to stop, the map must call that completion rather than failure."

Lin stared at the blank fourth wall.

The Cartography of Return might end with no return. A people informed of the buried history's existence could choose continued silence. Her work would have to preserve that choice even if it left Aren's lower thread inaccessible, the guild's crime unshared, and Mara's inheritance carried by a minority.

She felt resistance inside herself as physical as the fold's cold.

"You do not trust them to want," Sef said.

"I do not trust a desire manufactured by three generations of removal."

"Then you inherited the Founders' argument."

The sentence struck cleanly.

The Founders had not trusted citizens to carry grudges. The guild had not trusted them to reconsider peace. Lin did not trust them to choose memory after learning what it cost. Every version placed wise custodians above dangerous public desire.

She turned the map over and wrote on the back: No outcome required.

Mara read it. "Now begin."

The second stage offered recognition without full memory. Volunteers would receive names and faces from the clean-up registry, each paired with source and known uncertainty. No pain, fear, or final moments unless the person whose private record survived had given consent before removal. Where consent could not be established, the assembly could acknowledge a name without opening experience.

Aren belonged there.

Lin marked his entry with the same open hook used for uncertainty. The registry proved removal and lower continuation. It did not yet prove the contents of his buried line or his wishes. She could name him. She could not spend his experience to persuade the Hold.

Cael touched the hook. "You could ask him if the lower thread opens."

"If it opens. If a person remains capable of answer."

"And if he says no?"

"Then no."

The word hurt. It also held.

The third stage concerned shared history: the forged border, the House correspondence, the guild objections, the Suppression. These records would not enter private minds through the Weave. They would first appear as public artifacts in the Hall, the Archive, and ward houses. Citizens would read, dispute, and request particular witness clusters. Memory followed knowledge, not the reverse.

Pell arrived from the immediate-return faction during the afternoon. He read the third stage and accused Lin of translating history back into documents controlled by literate guild hands.

"A marsh worker cannot inspect provenance while hauling reed," he said. "The council delays truth by making it procedural."

"Then ward readers travel," Lin replied. "Public recitations. Copies held outside the Archive."

"And the fold?"

"Opened by request."

"You make grief wait in a queue."

Mara answered him. "A queue can oppress. So can pouring a dead stranger's terror into someone who asked only for facts."

Pell paced the narrow alcove. He represented impatience honestly. Corrin's faction might seize the delay and make it permanent. Every stage offered an obstruction point.

Lin added automatic review dates. If a closer halted the route, the reason became public within one day. The citizens' assembly reconvened within seven. A closure expired after thirty unless renewed by a new vote. No guild office could invoke safety without naming measurable harm.

Pell still disliked the map. He agreed it could move faster than permanent review.

Corrin came at dusk without invitation.

He stood outside the curtain until Lin admitted him. His ranked cord remained at his throat. He read the first two walls in silence.

"You cannot measure grievance load," he said.

"No."

"You cannot predict what private memory will do to the Weave."

"No."

"You do not know whether closing after a voluntary trial leaves the carrier's upper thread altered."

"No. That is why the trial includes a closer and seven days of observation."

His finger found the automatic expiry. "You make caution temporary by rule."

"I make indefinite concealment require repeated public consent."

"The public does not understand the substrate."

"The guild understood it and performed the Suppression. Expertise did not make us trustworthy."

Corrin flinched as if she had accused him personally.

Perhaps she had.

He walked to the blank wall. "Write what happens if the Hold chooses immediate release despite your stages."

Lin had not expected help from him. "The map closes at safe load."

"That gives the closer veto over the assembly."

"Then emergency closure lasts one hour. Continued closure requires the witness assembly and two substrate readers who disagree on rank allegiance."

"No reader outside the Hall has training."

"Teach one."

Corrin looked toward Sef's bed. "Before the old man loses the method?"

"Yes."

Urgency entered the map. Sef could train Lin, but a divided authority required more than one reader. They listed candidates from the Hall whose work showed accuracy and resistance to rank. Corrin named two Lin had assumed belonged to his faction.

"They do," he said. "A faction is not a skull."

The fourth wall filled after dark.

Unknown harms:

A carrier may retain memory after closure.

The Weave may mark refusal as damage.

Citizens may pressure relatives to choose one condition.

Employers may prefer the Silenced or the remembering.

Families may claim buried names for status.

The guild may turn instruction into priesthood.

The assembly may turn grief into loyalty tests.

The wider settlements may reject the Hold's ownership of the returned account.

For each harm they drew a response, and where no response existed, an open hook.

Lin tested those responses by assigning each person an interest hostile to the map. Pell argued as a ward that wanted immediate opening. Corrin argued for permanent closure. Mara spoke for an outer settlement refusing the Hold's claim to inherited testimony. Cael represented a carrier whose family demanded access. The Archivist took the part of an official ordered to disclose a private source. Sef, awake again, listened for any role no one had permitted to refuse.

Pell began with speed. "Three wards nominate representatives by noon. The fourth refuses because its well pump failed. Does the assembly wait?"

Lin pointed to the quorum clause. Four of seven wards had to seat witnesses.

"Then three wealthy wards keep a poor ward working until its absence blocks the vote," he said.

She revised quorum. Emergency labor could appoint a temporary witness selected by workers present, not by the council. Travel time became part of the schedule. No deadline could expire while a ward lacked water, fire control, or safe passage to deliberation.
